{"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-e14624ee-8d50-4193-bd76-4cb1fbe89e6c-12","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"1→\"\"\"Support-free counterbores: the stepped bridging hole.\n\nA counterbored hole prints mouth toward the bed, because that is where the screw head\ncomes from. That floats its ceiling: the shelf the head bears on is laid flat over the\nopen bore, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air. Slicers print it\nbadly or demand support inside a pocket nobody can clean.\n\nThe print-farm fix is sequential bridging, two sacrificial layers between bore and\nhole. First a slot the hole's width, bridged chord-to-chord across the bore; then the\n10→same slot turned ninety degrees, bridged across the first; then the round hole starts\nover the crossing, ringed by material that bridges the short way at every point. Each\nlayer spans only what the one before it laid, so the whole stack prints support-free\nand the screw never notices: the steps sit above the head's seat and every opening\nclears the shaft.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom build123d import Align, Box, Cylinder, Pos\n\n# How far the cutter reaches past its own mouth, so a caller seating the mouth flush\n20→# on the part's bottom face never leaves a coplanar-face boolean to the kernel's mood.\nLEAD = 1.0\n\n_SEATED = (Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN)\n\n\ndef counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth, layer=1.0):\n    \"\"\"The negative space of a counterbored hole whose ceiling prints without support.\n\n    Returns a cutter to subtract: mouth centred at the origin opening downward, hole\n30→    rising up +z, which is a counterbore printed the way one is used, head toward the\n    bed. `hole_dia` is the shaft's hole, `head_dia` and `head_depth` the head's pocket,\n    `depth` the full reach of the hole from the mouth. Position it at the part's bottom\n    face; the mouth runs 1mm long so a flush seat still cuts clean, and `depth` can\n    be generous for a through hole, the excess sticks out the top harmlessly.\n\n    Between pocket and hole sit two bridge steps, each `layer` tall: a slot across the\n    bore, then the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first. Anything at or\n    above the printed layer height bridges; the default is 1mm, exactly the printer's\n    min_wall, so the sacrificial laminae between the steps never read as thin walls to\n40→    the checker. Shrink it only when depth is tight, and expect min_wall to notice.\n    \"\"\"\n    if hole_dia <= 0 or head_dia <= 0 or head_depth <= 0 or layer <= 0:\n        raise ValueError(\"counterbore() needs positive dimensions throughout\")\n    if head_dia <= hole_dia:\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"a counterbore needs a head pocket wider than its hole, got head_dia \"\n            f\"{head_dia:g} against hole_dia {hole_dia:g}. For a plain hole, cut a Cylinder.\"\n        )\n    if depth < head_depth + 2 * layer + 1e-9:\n50→        raise ValueError(\n            f\"depth {depth:g} leaves no hole above the pocket: the head takes \"\n            f\"{head_depth:g} and the two bridge steps take {2 * layer:g}\"\n        )\n    pocket = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, head_depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n    # The steps stay inside the pocket's circle: a slot cut square would notch the wall.\n    inside = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, 2 * layer, align=_SEATED)\n    first = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Box(head_dia, hole_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    second = Pos(0, 0, head_depth + layer) * Box(hole_dia, head_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    shaft = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(hole_dia / 2, depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n60→    return pocket + first + second + shaft\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"ReadFile","FileContent":{"content":"1→\"\"\"Support-free counterbores: the stepped bridging hole.\n\nA counterbored hole prints mouth toward the bed, because that is where the screw head\ncomes from. First a slot the hole's width, bridged chord-to-chord across the bore; then the\nsame slot turned ninety degrees, bridged across the first; then the round hole starts\nover the crossing, ringed by material that bridges the short way at every point. Each\nlayer spans only what the one before it laid, so the whole stack prints support-free\nand the screw never notices: the steps sit above the head's seat and every opening\nclears the shaft.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom build123d import Align, Box, Cylinder, Pos\n\n# How far the cutter reaches past its own mouth, so a caller seating the mouth flush\n# on the part's bottom face never leaves a coplanar-face boolean to the kernel's mood.\nLEAD = 1.0\n\n_SEATED = (Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN)\n\n\ndef counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth, layer=1.0):\n    \"\"\"The negative space of a counterbored hole whose ceiling prints without support.\n\n    Returns a cutter to subtract: mouth centred at the origin opening downward, hole\n    rising up +z, which is a counterbore printed the way one is used, head toward the\n    bed. `hole_dia` is the shaft's hole, `head_dia` and `head_depth` the head's pocket,\n    `depth` the full reach of the hole from the mouth. Position it at the part's bottom\n    face; the mouth runs 1mm long so a flush seat still cuts clean, and `depth` can\n    be generous for a through hole, the excess sticks out the top harmlessly.\n\n    Between pocket and hole sit two bridge steps, each `layer` tall: a slot across the\n    bore, then the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first. Anything at or\n    above the printed layer height bridges; the default is 1mm, exactly the printer's\n    min_wall, so the sacrificial laminae between the steps never read as thin walls to\n    the checker. Shrink it only when depth is tight, and expect min_wall to notice.\n    \"\"\"\n    if hole_dia <= 0 or head_dia <= 0 or head_depth <= 0 or layer <= 0:\n        raise ValueError(\"counterbore() needs positive dimensions throughout\")\n    if head_dia <= hole_dia:\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"a counterbore needs a head pocket wider than its hole, got head_dia \"\n            f\"{head_dia:g} against hole_dia {hole_dia:g}. For a plain hole, cut a Cylinder.\"\n        )\n    if depth < head_depth + 2 * layer + 1e-9:\n        raise ValueError(\n            f\"depth {depth:g} leaves no hole above the pocket: the head takes \"\n            f\"{head_depth:g} and the two bridge steps take {2 * layer:g}\"\n        )\n    pocket = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, head_depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n    # The steps stay inside the pocket's circle: a slot cut square would notch the wall.\n    inside = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Cylinder(head_dia / 2, 2 * layer, align=_SEATED)\n    first = Pos(0, 0, head_depth) * Box(head_dia, hole_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    second = Pos(0, 0, head_depth + layer) * Box(hole_dia, head_dia, layer, align=_SEATED) & inside\n    shaft = Pos(0, 0, -LEAD) * Cylinder(hole_dia / 2, depth + LEAD, align=_SEATED)\n    return pocket + first + second + shaft\n","total_lines":61}},"locations":[]}

One edge that cannot land takes the whole call down, and\nthe natural response is to shrink the set by hand until it builds. That is how a part\nwith ninety exposed edges ends up with three chamfered ones, and it is why the same\ndoctrine produces far more chamfers in a GUI, where a human adds them one feature at a\ntime and works around each collision individually.\n\nThe doctrine describes this algorithm and used to leave every project to write it. It\n10→was 35 lines in one `system.py`, which meant the next project would write it again or,\nfar more likely, skip it and hand-narrow the set, which is exactly what the rule exists\nto prevent.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport collections.abc\nimport functools\n\nfrom build123d import chamfer as _chamfer\n\n20→# What OCCT says when a chamfer will not land. The same strings `nurb verify`\n# matches on, because both are asking the same question: did the kernel refuse, or did\n# the part refuse in its own words.\nKERNEL = (\"Failed creating a chamfer\", \"BRep_API\")\n\n\ndef _advice(count, size, word):\n    \"\"\"What the doctrine knows about this failure, said at the moment it happens.\n\n    The kernel's own message is \"try a smaller length value(s)\", and the doctrine is\n30→    explicit that following it would never have found the real rule: a chamfer fails\n    for room, not for length. Shrinking it is the one response that makes a part quietly\n    worse, because a 0.4mm chamfer lands and then reads as a defect on the print.\n\n    So the rule goes here rather than only in `nurb rules`. This is the most common way\n    a part stops building, and an error is the one place a reader is guaranteed to be\n    looking.\n    \"\"\"\n    lines = [\n        \"\",\n40→        f\"  A {word} fails for room, not for length, and shrinking it is usually the\",\n        f\"  wrong fix. Two {word}ed convex edges need more than {2 * size:g}mm of face between\",\n        f\"  them, and a single one needs more than {size:g}mm.\",\n    ]\n    if count > 1:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            f\"  This call passed {count} edges as one batch, and a batch is all or nothing:\",\n            \"  one edge that cannot land takes the whole call down. Every edge in it very\",\n            \"  likely chamfers fine on its own, so testing them one at a time will report\",\n50→            \"  that nothing is wrong.\",\n            \"\",\n            # `!r` not `:g`: a size is a measurement, and this codebase reads a bare `1`\n            # as a count. Suggested code has to echo back the float the caller passed.\n            f\"  polish(shape, edges, {size!r}) bisects the set for you and keeps the halves that\",\n            \"  build, which is what the doctrine prescribes instead of narrowing by hand.\",\n        ]\n    else:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n60→            \"  A single edge that fails at every length is the other case: its end lands on\",\n            \"  a vertex with four faces and only three edges between them, and OCCT has no\",\n            \"  cap for that corner. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-53aaae68-66af-42e5-8409-e97c1d3edf50-18","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"1349→@rule(\"min_wall\")\n1350→def min_wall(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"The thinnest section in the solid: a ray cast, corrected by an inscribed sphere\n    wherever the correction could change the verdict.\n\n    The ray is exact on the flat parallel walls that make up most of a printed part,\n    and an overestimate everywhere else: through a skewed wall it measures the slant,\n    up to 1/0.3 of the true section at its own exit filter's floor. So any chord thin\n    enough that the true section might still be under `min_wall` gets refined by\n    `_sphere_at`, which measures the way a wall is actually thin, perpendicular to\n    nothing in particular. The gate follows from the filter rather than being tuned: an\n1360→    accepted chord leaves within the 0.3 cosine floor, so a chord past `min_wall / 0.3`\n    cannot be hiding a failing section, and the sphere is only paid for where it could\n    matter. That also prices `min_wall = 0`, the card's way of saying \"this rule cannot\n    measure this part\", at nothing.\n\n    Two caveats survive the correction, and live on cards rather than in code:\n\n    - A taper reads as a wall. Near the tip of a designed knife edge every section is\n      short, sphere and chord alike. The gridfinity shelves' sockets measure 0.84mm\n      where their 45 degree walls meet, and their cards allow 0.7 next to the sentence\n1370→      saying why.\n    - A relief reads as a wall. The calibration coupon's raised label is 0.5mm proud,\n      and that is what gets measured, so its card sets `min_wall = 0`.\n\n    Probes sample faces, so a pinch nothing lands near is still missed. Treat a clean\n    result as \"no thin walls found\", not as \"no thin walls\".\n    \"\"\"\n    thinnest, spot = None, None\n    floor = ctx.min_wall * 0.01\n    for face in shape.faces():\n1380→        if face.area < ctx.sliver_area:\n            continue  # a face this small has no wall to speak of\n        for point, normal in _probes(face):\n            inward = -normal\n            hits = shape.find_intersection_points(Axis(point + inward * PROBE, inward))\n            # Only count a surface the ray leaves through: its outward normal points\n            # along the way the ray is going. A hit facing back at the ray means the\n            # ray had already left the material and struck something across a gap,\n            # which is how a chamfer two corners away reads as a thin wall.\n            reach = [\n1390→                (hit - point).length\n                for hit, hit_normal in hits\n                if (hit - point).dot(inward) > floor\n                and hit_normal.dot(inward) > 0.3\n            ]\n            if not reach:\n                continue\n            near = min(reach)\n            if near * 0.3 < ctx.min_wall:\n                # The sphere starts from the first boundary crossing rather than from\n1400→                # the accepted chord, because half of that is guaranteed to sit inside\n                # the material, and the shrink only ever moves down from there.\n                first = min(\n                    (hit - point).length for hit, _ in hits if (hit - point).length > floor\n                )\n                section = _sphere_at(shape, point, normal, first)\n                if section is not None and floor < section < near:\n                    near = section\n            if thinnest is None or near < thinnest:\n                thinnest, spot = near, point\n1410→    # Slack, because a card declaring the value it measured should not then fail on the\n    # last bit of a float. One percent rather than a micron: the same solid measures\n    # 0.500 against one OCCT build and 0.498 against another, and a threshold tight\n    # enough to see that turns a card into a claim about which machine wrote it. A card\n    # can also set `min_wall = 0` to say the rule cannot measure this part at all, which\n    # is honest for anything whose thinnest apparent section is not a wall.\n    if thinnest is None or thinnest >= ctx.min_wall * 0.99 - 1e-3:\n        return []\n    return [\n        Finding(\n1420→            \"min_wall\",\n            WARN,\n            f\"{thinnest:.2f}mm section, under the {ctx.min_wall:.2f}mm this printer \"\n            f\"lays down reliably\",\n            value=round(thinnest, 3),\n            where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in spot),\n        )\n    ]\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"ReadFile","FileContent":{"content":"1349→@rule(\"min_wall\")\n1350→def min_wall(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"The thinnest section in the solid: a ray cast, corrected by an inscribed sphere\n    wherever the correction could change the verdict.\n\n    The ray is exact on the flat parallel walls that make up most of a printed part,\n    and an overestimate everywhere else: through a skewed wall it measures the slant,\n    up to 1/0.3 of the true section at its own exit filter's floor. So any chord thin\n    enough that the true section might still be under `min_wall` gets refined by\n    `_sphere_at`, which measures the way a wall is actually thin, perpendicular to\n    nothing in particular. The gate follows from the filter rather than being tuned: an\n1360→    accepted chord leaves within the 0.3 cosine floor, so a chord past `min_wall / 0.3`\n    cannot be hiding a failing section, and the sphere is only paid for where it could\n    matter. That also prices `min_wall = 0`, the card's way of saying \"this rule cannot\n    measure this part\", at nothing.\n\n    Two caveats survive the correction, and live on cards rather than in code:\n\n    - A taper reads as a wall. Near the tip of a designed knife edge every section is\n      short, sphere and chord alike. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-cd47aaad-2333-40b7-85ee-9cbde3530bc4-23","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"620→@rule(\"overhang\")\ndef overhang(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Downward faces the printer cannot lay down unaided.\n\n    Angle is measured from the build direction, so a vertical wall is 0 and a flat\n    ceiling is 90. Two things are deliberately not findings: a face on the bed, which\n    is the first layer, and a short bridge, which a printer spans on its own. Warning\n    about either is how a checker gets switched off.\n    \"\"\"\n    up = Vector(*ctx.up).normalized()\n630→    bed, _ = _span(shape, up)\n    solid = shape.solids()[0] if shape.solids() else None\n    found = []\n    for face in shape.faces():\n        if _span(face, up)[1] <= bed + 1e-4:\n            continue  # grounded, so it is the first layer rather than an overhang\n        worst = max(\n            degrees(asin(max(-1.0, min(1.0, -n.dot(up))))) for n in _sample_normals(face)\n        )\n        if worst <= ctx.overhang_limit + 1e-6:\n640→            continue\n        crossing = _bridged(solid, face, up, ctx) if solid else None\n        if crossing is not None and crossing <= ctx.bridge_limit:\n            continue  # a bridge this printer walks across\n        # A polish chamfer that a diagonal stand turns to face the bed is a strip\n        # `size * 1.42` wide, the same figure concave_cosmetic uses, and it prints:\n        # the flanks close over it at 45 degrees. Measured at 1.32mm on a stood\n        # bracket whose flat print is clean.\n        droop = max(ctx.overhang_reach, 1.42 * 1.15 * ctx.cosmetic_chamfer)\n        if crossing is None and _reach(face, up) <= droop:\n650→            continue  # a ledge too shallow to droop, whatever its angle or length\n        if crossing is not None:\n            found.append(\n                Finding(\n                    \"overhang\",\n                    WARN,\n                    f\"{crossing:.0f}mm bridge over {face.area:.1f}mm2, \"\n                    f\"past the {ctx.bridge_limit:.0f}mm this printer spans\",\n                    plain=f\"a {crossing:.0f}mm gap to cross in mid-air; this printer \"\n                    f\"holds a span up to {ctx.bridge_limit:.0f}mm\",\n660→                    value=round(crossing, 1),\n                    where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in face.center()),\n                )\n            )\n        else:\n            found.append(\n                Finding(\n                    \"overhang\",\n                    FAIL,\n                    f\"{worst:.0f}deg unsupported over {face.area:.1f}mm2, \"\n670→                    f\"limit {ctx.overhang_limit:.0f}deg\",\n                    plain=f\"an underside leaning {worst:.0f} degrees out. Past \"\n                    f\"{ctx.overhang_limit:.0f} it droops as it prints\",\n                    value=round(worst, 1),\n                    where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in face.center()),\n                )\n            )\n    return found\n\n\n680→@rule(\"floating\")\ndef floating(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"A region whose first layer would be laid on air.\n\n    The overhang rule judges faces one at a time by angle, and a floating region can\n    present nothing steeper than the limit: stand an L on the end of one leg and the\n    other leg's tip hangs in space with every face at exactly 45 degrees. What gives\n    it away is the shape of the low point: a vertex above the bed with every edge\n    rising away from it. Somewhere to sit is what a first layer needs, and angle\n    cannot measure it.\n690→\n    Supported means one of two things, and both are needed. An edge running further\n    down: the second-lowest corner of a stood slab has air straight below it, but its\n    silhouette descends to the facet and every layer rests on the one before. Without this, a shallow depression reads as a thin section of whatever it is\n    pressed into: the scraper's detent dimple measured 1.07mm sitting in a 2.3mm web,\n    between two surfaces bounding the same air.\n    \"\"\"\n    inward = -outward\n    r = chord / 2\n    w = None\n    for _ in range(12):\n        center = point + inward * r\n        d, q, _ = shape.distance_to_with_closest_points(center)\n        if d >= r - 1e-5:\n            break\n        w = point - q\n        if w.length < 1e-6:\n            return None  # pinned at the tangent point, which is curvature, not a wall\n        denom = 2 * w.dot(outward)\n        if denom <= 1e-9:\n            return None  # the contact is behind the tangent plane, nothing to measure\n        grown = w.dot(w) / denom\n        if grown >= r - 1e-6:\n            break\n        r = grown\n    if w is not None and w.dot(w) / (2 * r * r) - 1 <= 0.3:\n        return None\n    return 2 * r\n\n\n@rule(\"min_wall\")\ndef min_wall(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"The thinnest section in the solid: a ray cast, corrected by an inscribed sphere\n    wherever the correction could change the verdict.\n\n    The ray is exact on the flat parallel walls that make up most of a printed part,\n    and an overestimate everywhere else: through a skewed wall it measures the slant,\n    up to 1/0.3 of the true section at its own exit filter's floor. So any chord thin\n    enough that the true section might still be under `min_wall` gets refined by\n    `_sphere_at`, which measures the way a wall is actually thin, perpendicular to\n    nothing in particular. The gate follows from the filter rather than being tuned: an\n","total_lines":1428}},"locations":[]}

The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. It is the most common way a\n# part stops building, so it is the one message worth owning. `fillet` is deliberately\n# not wrapped: build123d's fillet error already names `max_fillet()` and a smaller\n# radius really is the fix there, so the chamfer rule would be wrong half the time.\n# `import_stl` shadows build123d's for a blunter reason: build123d's returns a `Face`,\n# a sheet of triangles with no inside, and subtracting from one segfaults instead of\n# raising. A part builds inside the `nurb dev` process, so the whole watcher dies\n60→# without printing anything. It is also the first name a model tries when a downloaded\n# file lands in a project. The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. pockets are this plus 0.3mm of clearance\n    columns: number of bit pockets along the long side\n    \"\"\"\n    if columns < 1:\n        reject(\"columns must be at least 1\", param=\"columns\")\n    if shank_diameter <= 0:\n        reject(\"shank_diameter must be greater than 0\", param=\"shank_diameter\")\n\n    pocket_dia = shank_diameter + _CLEARANCE\n    pocket_r = pocket_dia / 2\n    pitch = pocket_dia + _WALL\n    margin = _WALL + pocket_r\n    width = 2 * margin + (columns - 1) * pitch\n    depth = 2 * margin + (_ROWS - 1) * pitch\n    height = _POCKET_DEPTH + _FLOOR\n\n    # Vertical sides and a sharp bed face; the 0.8mm top rim is lofted on so the\n    # corner is a miter rather than a sliver triangle from chamfer().\n    base = Box(width, depth, height - _LEAD_IN, align=_SEATED)\n    cap = loft(\n        [\n            Pos(0, 0, height - _LEAD_IN) * Face.make_rect(width, depth),\n            Pos(0, 0, height) * Face.make_rect(width - 2 * _LEAD_IN, depth - 2 * _LEAD_IN),\n        ],\n        ruled=True,\n    )\n    body = base + cap\n\n    extra = 1.0\n    cutter = None\n    for col in range(columns):\n        for row in range(_ROWS):\n            x = -width / 2 + margin + col * pitch\n            y = -depth / 2 + margin + row * pitch\n            # Cylinder through the top so the mouth is open; cone is the 0.8 x 45\n            # lead-in and overshoots the top face so the boolean is not coplanar.\n            shaft = Pos(x, y, _FLOOR) * Cylinder(\n                pocket_r, _POCKET_DEPTH + extra, align=_SEATED\n            )\n            cone = Pos(x, y, height - _LEAD_IN) * Cone(\n                pocket_r,\n                pocket_r + _LEAD_IN + extra,\n                _LEAD_IN + extra,\n                align=_SEATED,\n            )\n            bit = shaft + cone\n            cutter = bit if cutter is None else cutter + bit\n    body = body - cutter\n\n    # Lead-ins are functional, not polish; draft does not drop them.\n    return body\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

The floor under the pockets is 3mm, so the block is 15mm tall. Each mouth and the top outer rim get a 0.8mm x 45° lead-in; the bottom stays sharp so the bounding box is the block itself.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not polish the bottom perimeter: the bed face has to stay the full 43.5 x 18.6.\n- Do not `chamfer()` the top outer edges: two 0.8mm chamfers meeting at a corner leave a ~0.32mm² triangle, which fails the sliver check. Loft the rim instead.\n- Do not accept check findings on the card; the geometry has to pass.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-24: first cut, 5x2 pockets from the measured 6.0mm shank.\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}
